\begin{split}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}&=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}}\right)F_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{i+1}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{t}\sigma_{i}+[z^{\pm},\sigma_{i}]}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{i-1}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0},\end{split}

in particular, the Lie-transport of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}
is a sum of terms satisfying the key assumptions of the first part of the Lemma with modified sequences of
\sigma_{i}
and
F_{0}
. The assumed bounds on
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\varsigma_{i},\ \mathcal{A}^{\pm}a_{i}
and
(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}})Y
and the first part of the Lemma guarantee that:

\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}}\right)F_{0}\simeq a_{1,k}\Psi^{*}(\phi_{1,k+1}\nu)+a_{2,k}\Psi^{1*}(\phi_{2,k+1}k)+\Psi^{*}(\phi_{3,k+1})Y_{k+1}

with

\begin{split}&||a_{1,k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}L^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(r+k)A_{1,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1,\\
&||a_{2,k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{r+k-1}L_{\mathcal{A}}(k-1+r)A_{3,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1,\\
&||Y_{k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}L_{\mathcal{A}}(r+k)A_{3,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1.\\
\end{split}

Similarly, applying the first part of the Lemma, we get:

\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{j+1}}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{t}\sigma_{j}+[z^{\pm},\sigma_{j}]}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{j-1}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}\simeq a_{1,k}\Psi^{*}(\phi_{1,k}\nu)+a_{2,k}\Psi^{1*}(\phi_{2,k}k)+\Psi^{*}(\phi_{3,k})Y_{k}

with

\begin{split}&||a_{1,k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}L^{1}(r+k)A_{1}\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1,\\
&||a_{2,k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{r+k-1}L(r+k-1)A_{3}\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1,\\
&||Y_{k}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}L(r+k)A_{3}\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1.\\
\end{split}

Gathering the above, we conclude that:

\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}\simeq a_{1,k,\mathcal{A}}\Psi^{*}(\phi_{1,k}\nu)+a_{2,k,\mathcal{A}}\Psi^{1*}(\phi_{2,k}k)+\Psi^{*}(\phi_{3,k})Y_{k,\mathcal{A}}

with

\begin{split}&||a_{1,k,\mathcal{A}}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}\left[L^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(k+r)A_{1,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}+L^{1}(k+r)A_{1}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,k\}}\left[\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\right]\right],\\
&||a_{2,k,\mathcal{A}}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q+1}\lambda_{q}^{r+k-1}\left[L_{\mathcal{A}}(r+k-1)A_{3,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}+L(r+k-1)A_{3}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,k\}}\left[\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\right]\right],\\
&||Y_{k,\mathcal{A}}||_{r}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r+k}\left[L_{\mathcal{A}}(k+r)A_{3,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}+L(k+r)A_{3}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,k\}}\left[\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\right]\right]\end{split}

for
0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-k-1,\ k\geq 1
.

Eventually, making
n_{2}
larger, we can use the same definitions for the constants
C,\ C^{\prime},\ C^{\prime\prime}
, given in (
8.15
).
∎

Remark 29

(Inductive Lemma without the Fast Coefficients)
.

We want to have a version of Lemma
8.1
to use in Gabrun’s stage. In that setting, there are no fast coefficients, and we do not need to require any geometry on the vector fields
\sigma_{i}
and the 1-form
F_{0}
. We can directly assume:

\begin{split}&||\partial_{t}^{j}F_{0}||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r}L(r+j)A\ \text{ for }j=0,1,2\ \text{ and }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-j,\\
&||\partial_{t}^{j}\sigma_{i}||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r}L^{\varsigma}(r+j)\ \bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }j=0,1,2\ \text{ and }\ \text{ for }0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-j,\\
&||(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}})F_{0}||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r}L_{\mathcal{A}}(r)A_{\mathcal{A}}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-1,\\
&||(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}})\sigma_{i}||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim\lambda_{q}^{r}L^{\varsigma}(r)\ \bar{\varsigma}_{i,\mathcal{A}}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq\bar{N}-j\\
\end{split}

with the same properties for
L,\ L^{\varsigma},\ L_{\mathcal{A}}
as in the Standing Assumptions
2
. A simple induction argument then leads to the estimate:

||\partial_{t}^{j}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}||_{r+\alpha}\leq C^{\prime}(C)^{k}\lambda_{q}^{r+k+j}L(r+k+j)A\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\ \text{ for }\ j=0,1,2\ \text{ and }\ 0\leq r+k+j\leq\bar{N}

for some constants
C,\ C^{\prime}
which depend on
\bar{C},\ \bar{N}
but not on
r,\ k,\ j
.
The rewriting (
A.17
) and another induction argument then gives:

||(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{z^{\pm}})\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{k}}\dots\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}}F_{0}||_{r+\alpha}\leq C^{\prime}(C)^{k}\lambda_{q}^{r+k}\left[L_{\mathcal{A}}(k+r)A_{\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}+L(k+r)A\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,k\}}\left[\bar{\varsigma}_{j,\mathcal{A}}\prod_{i=1,\dots,k\ i\neq j}\bar{\varsigma}_{i}\right]\right]

for
0\leq r+k\leq\bar{N}-1
and some possibly different constants
C,\ C^{\prime}
which depend on
\bar{C},\ \bar{N}
but not on
r,\ k
.

Appendix A
Tools from Differential Geometry

Vector calculus.
Let

U\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}\text{ or }U\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}

be open, and let

a=a^{i},\quad b=b^{i},\quad F=F^{i},\quad v=v^{i}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

be smooth vector fields, and

A=A^{ij}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}

a smooth matrix field. We use the following conventions for differential operators:

(A.1a)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{D}a)^{ij}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}a^{i},

(A.1b)

\displaystyle(b\cdot\nabla a)^{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{D}a[b])^{i}=b^{j}\partial_{j}a^{i},

(A.1c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}a

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}a^{i},

(A.1d)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}A)^{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}A^{ij},

together with the identities

(A.2a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=b\cdot\nabla a+(\mathrm{div}b)\,a,

(A.2b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl}(a\times b)

\displaystyle=b\cdot\nabla a-a\cdot\nabla b+(\mathrm{div}b)\,a-(\mathrm{div}a)\,b.

Additionally, we have:

(A.3)

\mathrm{div}[(F\times\nabla v)^{\top}]=\mathrm{curl}[F]\cdot\nabla v

where

(F\times\nabla v)^{ij}=\varepsilon^{ikl}F^{k}\partial_{l}v^{j}

and
\varepsilon^{ikl}
is the standard Levi-Civita tensor.

This can be proved as follows:

(A.4)

\begin{split}\left(\mathrm{div}\left[(F\times\nabla v)^{\top}\right]\right)^{i}&=\partial_{j}(\varepsilon^{jkl}F^{k}\partial_{l}v^{i})\\
&=\varepsilon^{jkl}\partial_{j}F^{k}\partial_{l}v^{i}+\varepsilon^{jkl}F^{k}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}v^{i}\\
&=\left(\mathrm{curl}[F]\cdot\nabla v\right)^{i}\end{split}

where we used the definition of curl

\varepsilon^{jkl}\partial_{j}F^{k}\partial_{l}v^{i}=\varepsilon^{ljk}\partial_{j}F^{k}\partial_{l}v^{i}=(\mathrm{curl}F)^{l}\partial_{l}v^{i}

and

\varepsilon^{jkl}F^{k}\partial_{j}\partial_{l}v^{i}=0

which follows from the anti-symmetry of the Levi-Civita tensor, the symmetry of the second-order derivatives

\varepsilon^{jkl}=\varepsilon^{ljk}=-\varepsilon^{lkj}\ \text{ and }\ \partial_{j}\partial_{l}v^{i}=\partial_{l}\partial_{j}v^{i}

and summing over the dummy indices.

Differential Geometry.
For any vector valued map
F:U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, scalar function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
, vector field
v:U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and diffeomorphism
\Psi:U^{\prime}\to U
, where
U,U^{\prime}
are open subsets of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we set the following notation conventions:

•

The pullback along
\Psi
of
F
as a vector field is given by:

\Psi^{*}F=(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}F(\Psi).

•

The pullback along
\Psi
of
f
is given by:

\Psi^{*}f=f(\Psi).

•

The pullback along
\Psi
of
F
as as a 1-form is given by:

\Psi^{1*}F=(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{T}F(\Psi).

•

The pullback along
\Psi
of
F
as a 2-form is given by:

\Psi^{2*}F=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi](\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}F(\Psi).

•

The pushforward along
\Psi
of
F
as a vector field is given by:

\Psi_{*}F=(\mathrm{D}\Psi)(\Psi^{-1})F(\Psi^{-1})=(\Psi^{-1})^{*}F.

•

The ‘Lie-derivative’ of
F
as a vector field in the direction
v
, that is, the commutator of
F
with a vector field
v
, is given by:

\mathcal{L}_{v}F=[v,F]=v\cdot\nabla F-F\cdot\nabla v.

•

The Lie-derivative of
F
as a 1-form in the direction
v
is given by:

\mathcal{L}^{1}_{v}F=\mathrm{curl}[F]\times v+\nabla(F\cdot v)=v\cdot\nabla F+\mathrm{D}v^{\top}F.

•

The Lie-derivative of
F
as a 2-form in the direction
v
is given by:

\mathcal{L}^{2f}_{v}F=\mathrm{curl}[F\times v]+(\mathrm{div}\ F)v=[v,F]+(\mathrm{div}\ v)F.

Even when the map
\Psi
is time-dependent, we always consider the pullbacks and Lie derivatives in space for each fixed time.

We use the above notation because, in this work, we will use the identifications coming from the well-known commutative diagram:

(A.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\flat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=*\flat}

together with the Hodge isomorphism

*:\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\to\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})

where the metric considered is the trivial one. In particular, a map
F:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
(possibly defined on an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{T}^{3}
only) can be thought of as either a 1-form
F\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, a 2-form
F\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
or a vector field
F\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
. We want, however, to take advantage of the following standard identities, see
[
32
, Chapters 12-14]
and
[
36
, Chapter 2]
, for differential forms
a,\ b
and vector fields
v,\ w
:

(A.6a)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}\mathrm{d}=\mathrm{d}\Psi^{*},

(A.6b)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}a\wedge\Psi^{*}b=\Psi^{*}(a\wedge b),

(A.6c)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}\mathcal{L}_{v}a=\mathcal{L}_{\Psi^{*}v}\Psi^{*}a,

(A.6d)

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}\iota_{v}a=\iota_{\Psi^{*}v}\Psi^{*}a,

(A.6e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}a=\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}a+\mathrm{d}\iota_{v}a,

(A.6f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}\mathcal{L}_{w}-\mathcal{L}_{w}\mathcal{L}_{v}=\mathcal{L}_{[v,w]}.

Despite the notation, these depend on the degree of the forms considered; thus, the identifications in (
A.5
) need to be done carefully. This explains why we distinguish in what sense the map
F
is being considered and add indices in the notation above accordingly. We now verify that the definitions and notational conventions are consistent with the identifications.

Vector Fields vs 2-Forms.
From the correspondence between 2-forms and vector fields in (
A.5
), namely

X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\iff\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=*X^{\flat}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3}),

the key identities in (
A.6
) and
\Psi^{*}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]\mathrm{d}\text{vol}
we compute:

\begin{split}\Psi^{*}\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=\iota_{\Psi^{*}X}(\Psi^{*}\mathrm{d}\text{vol})=\iota_{\Psi^{*}X}(\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]\mathrm{d}\text{vol})=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]\iota_{\Psi^{*}X}(\mathrm{d}\text{vol})=\iota_{\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]\Psi^{*}X}(\mathrm{d}\text{vol})\end{split}

and deduce that

\Psi^{2*}F=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi](\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}F(\Psi).

Moreover, given the geometric characterisation of divergence:

\mathrm{d}\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=\mathcal{L}_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=(\mathrm{div}\ X)\mathrm{d}\text{vol},

the identity

(A.7)

\iota_{v}\iota_{w}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}=(w\times v)^{\flat},

which can be easily verified and the commutativity of the diagram (
A.5
), we deduce that:

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{v}(\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol})&=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{v}\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol})+\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol})\\
&=\mathrm{d}((X\times v)^{\flat})+(\mathrm{div}\ X)\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}\\
&=\iota_{\mathrm{curl}[X\times v]}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}+\iota_{(\mathrm{div}\ X)v}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}\\
&=\iota_{\mathrm{curl}[X\times v]+(\mathrm{div}\ X)v}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}\\
&=\iota_{[v,X]+(\mathrm{div}\ v)X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol},\end{split}

and we conclude:

\mathcal{L}^{2f}_{v}F=[v,F]+(\mathrm{div}\ v)F.

Vector Fields vs 1-Forms.
Recall that:

X^{\flat}v=X\cdot v\ \leadsto\ \iota_{v}X^{\flat}=X^{\flat}v=X\cdot v.

Now, from the commutative diagram (
A.5
), Cartan’s Formula in (
A.6
) and (
A.7
) we get:

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{v}X^{\flat}&=\mathrm{d}\iota_{v}X^{\flat}+\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}X^{\flat}\\
&=\mathrm{d}(X\cdot v)+\iota_{v}\iota_{\mathrm{curl}X}\mathrm{d}\text{vol}\\
&=[\nabla(X\cdot v)]^{\flat}+(\mathrm{curl}X\times v)^{\flat}\\
&=[\nabla(X\cdot v)+\mathrm{curl}X\times v]^{\flat}.\end{split}

Concerning the pullback, we have:

(\Psi^{*}X^{\flat})(v)=X^{\flat}(\Psi)(\mathrm{D}\Psi[v])=X(\Psi)\cdot\mathrm{D}\Psi[v]=(\mathrm{D}\Psi^{\top}X(\Psi))\cdot v=(\mathrm{D}\Psi^{\top}X(\Psi))^{\flat}v

and we conclude that:

\Psi^{1*}F=\mathrm{D}\Psi^{\top}F(\Psi)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathcal{L}_{v}^{1}F=\nabla(F\cdot v)+\mathrm{curl}[F]\times v.

Products.
From the fact that:

Ma\times Mb=\det[M]M^{-\top}[a\times b],

we deduce that:

(A.8)

\begin{split}\Psi^{2*}F\times\Psi^{*}G&=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]\left[(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}F(\Psi)\times(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}G(\Psi)\right]=(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{\top}(F\times G)(\Psi)=\Psi^{1*}(F\times G),\\
\Psi^{1*}F\times\Psi^{1*}G&=\left[(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{T}F(\Psi)\times(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{T}G(\Psi)\right]=\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi](\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}[F(\Psi)\times G(\Psi)]=\Psi^{2*}(F\times G).\end{split}

We remark that these identities are nothing but an instance of

\Psi^{*}a\wedge\Psi^{*}b=\Psi^{*}(a\wedge b)

from (
A.6
) above.

A related property is:

(A.9)

\Psi^{1*}X\cdot\Psi^{*}Y=[(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{\top}X(\Psi)]\cdot[(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}Y(\Psi)]=X(\Psi)^{\top}\mathrm{D}\Psi(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{-1}Y(\Psi)=\Psi^{*}(X\cdot Y),

this is just the standard action of diffeomorphisms on the pairing between 1-forms and vector fields.

Some useful identities.
From the above we deduce the following identities:

(A.10a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl}\Psi^{1*}=\mathrm{d}\Psi^{*}=\Psi^{*}\mathrm{d}=\Psi^{2*}\mathrm{curl},

(A.10b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\ \Psi^{2*}=\mathrm{d}\Psi^{*}=\Psi^{*}\mathrm{d}=\Psi^{*}\mathrm{div},

(A.10c)

\displaystyle\det[\mathrm{D}\Psi]=1\Longrightarrow\Psi^{*}=\Psi^{2*},

(A.10d)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\ v=0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2f}_{v}=\mathcal{L}_{v}\ \text{ and }\ \mathrm{curl}[\mathcal{L}_{v}^{1}\Theta]=\mathcal{L}_{v}\mathrm{curl}[\Theta]

where the last identity follows from the definitions and the following computation:

(A.11)

\begin{split}\mathrm{curl}[\mathcal{L}_{v}^{1}\Theta]&=\mathrm{d}\left(\mathrm{d}\iota_{v}\Theta+\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}\Theta\right)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\left(\iota_{v}\mathrm{d}\Theta\right)\\
&=\mathrm{curl}\left[\mathrm{curl}\Theta\times v\right]\\
&=\left[v,\mathrm{curl}\Theta\right]\\
&=\mathcal{L}_{v}\left[\mathrm{curl}\Theta\right].\end{split}

In particular, if
\xi=\mathrm{curl}\ \Theta
and
v,\ B
are divergence-free vector fields, we have:

(A.12)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{v})\xi=\mathrm{curl}\left[(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}^{1}_{v})\Theta\right],\\
\mathcal{L}_{B}\xi=\mathrm{curl}[\mathcal{L}^{1}_{B}\Theta].\end{cases}

Lie-Transport Equations.
We recall the following standard identity; see
[
36
, Chapter 2]
for a proof. Let
M
be either
\mathbb{T}^{3}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and let
\xi=\xi(x,s)
be a smooth time-dependent vector field on
M
. Let
X_{s}
be the associated flow map, namely the solution of

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}X_{s}(x)=\xi(X_{s}(x),s),\\
X_{0}(x)=x\end{cases}

then, for any smooth time-dependent tensor field

F(\cdot,s)\in\Gamma\bigl(M,(TM)^{\otimes p}\otimes(T^{*}M)^{\otimes q}\bigr)

one has

(A.13)

\partial_{s}(X_{s}^{*}F)=X_{s}^{*}\bigl(\partial_{s}F+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}F\bigr)

where the Lie derivative is understood depending on
(p,q)
.

Duhamel Formula for Lie-Transport.
From the identity (
A.13
) follows that for any time-independent smooth differential form
F_{0}
, we have

(A.14)

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}F+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}F=G\\
F|_{s=0}=F_{0}\end{cases}\iff F(\cdot,s)=\Phi_{s}^{*}F_{0}+\int_{0}^{s}\Phi_{s}^{*}X_{s^{\prime}}^{*}[G(\cdot,s^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}

where
\Phi_{s}=X_{s}^{-1}
is the inverse flow map of
\xi
, and we implicitly assume that both sides are well defined.

Indeed assume that
F
solves the initial value problem. From (
A.13
) we obtain:

\partial_{s}(X_{s}^{*}F)=X_{s}^{*}[\partial_{s}F+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}F]=X_{s}^{*}G.

Integrating this expression from 0 to
s
, we deduce:

X_{s}^{*}F-F_{0}=\int_{0}^{s}X_{s^{\prime}}^{*}G(\cdot,s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

now applying
\Phi_{s}^{*}=(X_{s}^{-1})^{*}
we conclude that:

F(\cdot,s)=\Phi_{s}^{*}F_{0}+\int_{0}^{s}\Phi_{s}^{*}X_{s^{\prime}}^{*}G(\cdot,s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

The converse implication follows by proceeding backward, applying
X^{*}_{s}
to the Duhamel formula, taking
\partial_{s}
derivative and using (
A.13
) we see that:

X^{*}_{s}(\partial_{s}F+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}F)=X^{*}_{s}G.

But
X_{s}
is a diffeomorphism, and the solution property follows. The fact that it has the right initial condition is immediate from the formula.

Lagrangian Flow vs Transport.
From the above, applied to the scalar case, we obtain:

(A.15)

\begin{cases}\partial_{s}X_{s}=\xi(X_{s},s),\\
X_{0}=\mathrm{Id}\end{cases}\ \ \ \ \overset{\Phi_{s}=X_{s}^{-1}}{\iff}\ \ \ \ \begin{cases}\partial_{s}\Phi_{s}+\xi(\cdot,s)\cdot\nabla\Phi_{s}=0,\\
\Phi_{0}=\mathrm{Id}.\end{cases}

Pullback vs Lie Derivation.
Let
T
be any smooth tensor field and
v
any smooth vector field. We will show that:

\Upsilon^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{v}T)=\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon^{*}v}(\Upsilon^{*}T).

We first recall that for any time-independent diffeomorphism
\Upsilon
, one has:

X^{v}_{s}\circ\Upsilon=\Upsilon\circ X^{\Upsilon^{*}v}_{s},

where we denote by
X^{v}_{s}
the Lagrangian flows of
v
at time
s
starting at the identity at time
0
. In our case,
v
will depend on time, but the differentiation in the Lie derivative happens at each fixed time, i.e. we freeze the time
t
in
v
and compute
X^{v}_{s}=X^{v|_{t}}_{s}
thinking of
v
as
v|_{t}
, a time-independent object. With this in mind, the definition of Lie derivative and the fact that
\Psi^{*}\Upsilon^{*}=(\Upsilon\circ\Psi)^{*}
, we compute:

(A.16)

\begin{split}\Upsilon^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{v}T)&=\Upsilon^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{v|_{t}}T)\\
&=\Upsilon^{*}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(X^{v|_{t}}_{s})^{*}T\right)\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(X^{v|_{t}}_{s}\circ\Upsilon)^{*}T\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(\Upsilon\circ X^{\Upsilon^{*}v|_{t}}_{s})^{*}T\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}(X^{\Upsilon^{*}{v|_{t}}}_{s})^{*}(\Upsilon^{*}T)\\
&=\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon^{*}{v|_{t}}}(\Upsilon^{*}T)\\
&=\mathcal{L}_{\Upsilon^{*}v}(\Upsilon^{*}T)\end{split}

and the commutation is proven.

Transport Trick.
From the key commutation relation:

\mathcal{L}_{F}\mathcal{L}_{G}-\mathcal{L}_{G}\mathcal{L}_{F}=\mathcal{L}_{[F,G]}

in (
A.6
), we deduce a useful identity that can be used to deal with the Alfvén Lie-transport of high-order terms in the Lie-Taylor expansions, namely

(A.17)

\begin{split}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{v}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k}F&=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{v}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k-1}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{v}\right)\Theta+\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{t}\xi+[v,\xi]}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k-1}F\\
&=\dots\\
&=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k}\left(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{v}\right)F+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{i}\mathcal{L}_{\partial_{t}\xi+[v,\xi]}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k-1-i}F,\\
\end{split}

Similarly,

\mathcal{L}_{B}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k}F=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{B}F+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{i}\mathcal{L}_{[B,\xi]}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}^{k-1-i}F.

Minimal covers.
For
\rho>0
a finite open covering
\mathcal{C}
of a compact manifold
\mathcal{M}
of dimension
n
is a minimal cover of diameter
\rho
with granularity
c_{0}
if:

•

The diameter of each
U_{j}\in\mathcal{C}
is less than
\rho
.

•

\mathcal{C}
can be subdivided into
n+1
subfamilies
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, each consisting of pairwise disjoint sets.

•

U,U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}\Longrightarrow\text{dist}(U,U^{\prime})>c_{0}
.

The following Proposition is a standard fact. We refer to
[
33
, Section 5.5]
.

Proposition A.1

(Minimal Coverings)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact manifold and
\rho>0
. Then
\mathcal{M}
admits a minimal cover
\mathcal{C}
of diameter
\rho
. Moreover, there exists a partition of unity
\{\theta_{j}\}
subordinate to
\mathcal{C}
, satisfying:

\sum_{j}\theta_{j}=1,\ \theta_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{j}),\ \theta_{j}\geq 0,\ ||\theta_{j}||_{r}\lesssim\rho^{-r}

where the implicit constant depends on
r
and the specific choice of the covering.

Note that
c_{0}
always exists by compactness, but for our purposes, an explicit control is needed; we will rely on the following explicit construction on
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, where we set
\rho=4/3\tau^{c}
and
c_{0}=1/3\tau^{c}
.

We think of
\mathbb{T}^{3}
as a periodic unit cube in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We now fix the cube:

Q_{0}=(0,2/3)^{3}

and let
\mathcal{F}_{0}
be the family of cubes given by 1-periodically translating
Q_{0}
. We construct three other periodic families by shifting this initial one in the coordinate directions, namely

\mathcal{F}_{l}=\mathcal{F}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}e_{l}\ \ \text{ for }\ \ l=1,2,3.

The union
\mathcal{C}=\cup_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}
gives a periodic tiling of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that:

(1)

U\in\mathcal{C}\Longrightarrow\text{diam}(U)=2/3\sqrt{3}
.

(2)

U,U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{l}\Longrightarrow\text{dist}(U,U^{\prime})\geq 1/3
.

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is minimal in the sense that each point in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is contained in at most four elements of
\mathcal{C}
.

For each
U_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{C}
standard techniques allow to construct a family
\{\theta_{j^{\prime}}\}
of cut-off function with
\theta_{j^{\prime}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{j^{\prime}},[0,1])
and such that:

(1)

\sum_{j^{\prime}}\theta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}\equiv 1
, in particular for each point
\mathbb{T}^{3}
at most four
\theta_{j^{\prime}}
are non-zero.

(2)

U_{j^{\prime}_{1}},U_{j^{\prime}_{2}}\in\mathcal{F}_{l}\Longrightarrow\text{dist}(\text{supp}(\theta_{j_{1}^{\prime}}),\ \text{supp}(\theta_{j^{\prime}_{2}}))>\text{dist}(U_{j_{1}^{\prime}},\ U_{j^{\prime}_{2}})\geq 1/3

and

\text{diam}\ \text{supp}\ \theta_{j^{\prime}}<\text{diam}(U_{j^{\prime}})\leq 2/3\sqrt{3}\leq 4/3.

(3)

||\theta_{j^{\prime}}||_{r}\lesssim 1
the implicit constant depends on
r
and the specific choice of the partition of unity.

Appropriately rescaling the construction by
\tau^{c}
, so that it is still periodic, we can achieve:

\text{diam}\ \text{supp}\ \theta_{j^{\prime}}<4/3\tau^{c}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ U_{j^{\prime}_{1}},U_{j^{\prime}_{2}}\in\mathcal{F}_{l}\Longrightarrow\text{dist}(\text{supp}(\theta_{j_{1}^{\prime}}),\ \text{supp}(\theta_{j^{\prime}_{2}}))>1/3\tau^{c}

while the other properties now read:

\sum_{j^{\prime}}\theta_{j^{\prime}}^{2}\equiv 1\ \ \text{ and }\ \ ||\theta_{j^{\prime}}||_{r}\lesssim(\tau^{c})^{-r}.

Appendix B
Tools from Analysis

Hölder Functions.
Let
r\geq 0
be an integer and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, for a possibly time dependent function
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote:

\begin{split}&||f||_{0}=\sup_{x}|f(x)|\ \text{ or }\ \sup_{x,t}|f(x,t)|,\\
&[f]_{r}=\sup_{|\theta|=r}||\partial_{\theta}f||\ \text{ or }\ \sup_{t}\sup_{|\theta|=r}||\partial_{\theta}f(\cdot,t)||,\\
&[f]_{r+\alpha}=\sup_{|\theta|=r}\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|\partial_{\theta}f(x)-\partial_{\theta}f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}\ \text{ or }\ \sup_{t}\sup_{|\theta|=r}\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|\partial_{\theta}f(x,t)-\partial_{\theta}f(y,t)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}},\\
&||f||_{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r}[f]_{j}\ \text{ and }\ ||f||_{r+\alpha}=||f||_{r}+[f]_{r+\alpha}\end{split}

where
\theta
denotes a multi-index, and with a slight abuse of notation, we keep the same notation for vector- and matrix-valued functions.

The following product and interpolation estimates are classical:

(B.1)

||fg||_{r}\lesssim||f||_{r}||g||_{0}+||f||_{0}||g||_{r}

where
r
is a non-negative real number, and the implicit constants depend on
r
, and

(B.2)

||f||_{r}\lesssim||f||_{r^{\prime}}^{\lambda}||f||_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{1-\lambda}\ \text{ for }\ r=\lambda r^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)r^{\prime\prime}\\

where
r,\ r^{\prime},\ r^{\prime\prime},\ \lambda
are non-negative real numbers and the implicit constants depend on
r,r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime},\lambda
.

Miscellaneous.
We now recall some standard results in Analysis. We give the formulations from
[
32
, Appendix C.34]
.

Proposition B.1

(Inverse Function Theorem)
.

Suppose
U
and
V
are open subsets
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
F:U\to V
is a smooth function. If
\mathrm{D}F(x_{0})
is invertible at some point

x_{0}\in U
, then there exist connected neighbourhoods
U_{0}\subseteq U
of
x_{0}
and
V_{0}\subseteq V
of
F(x_{0})
such that
F|_{U_{0}}:U_{0}\to V_{0}
is a diffeomorphism.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space. A map
G:X\to X
is said to be a contraction if there is a constant
c<1
such that
d(G(x),G(y))\leq c\ d(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
. Clearly, every contraction is continuous. A fixed point of a map
G:X\to X
is a point
x\in X

such that
G(x)=x
.

Proposition B.2

(Contraction Mapping Theorem)
.

Let
X
be a nonempty complete metric space.
Every contraction
G:X\to X
has a unique fixed point.

We now recall the following composition estimate; the proof is a simple adaptation of the corresponding result in
[
14
]
to the fractional case.

Proposition B.3

(Composition Estimates)
.

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\Omega
be two functions with
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then for any
0\leq\alpha<1
and
r\geq 0
integer, we have:

(B.3a)

\displaystyle[f\circ g]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq[f]_{\alpha}[g]_{1}^{\alpha},

(B.3b)

\displaystyle[f\circ\Psi]_{r+\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left([f]_{1+\alpha}[\Psi]_{r}+[f]_{r+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{r}\right)[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}+[f]_{1}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}+[f]_{r}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r}\ \text{ for }\ r\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}

where the implicit constants depend on
r,\ N,\ n,\ \alpha
.

Proof of
B.3
.

Following
[
14
]
, we can write the Fa’ di Bruno’s formula symbolically as:

(B.4)

\mathrm{D}^{r}(f\circ\Psi)=\sum_{l=1}^{r}(\mathrm{D}^{l}f)\circ\Psi\sum_{\sigma}C_{l,\sigma}(\mathrm{D}\Psi)^{\sigma_{1}}\dots(\mathrm{D}^{r}\Psi)^{\sigma_{r}}

for some constant
C_{l,\sigma}
, where the inner sum is on all the multi indexes
\sigma
with:

\sum_{j=1}^{r}j\sigma_{j}=r\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}=l,

from standard interpolation inequalities, we have for
j\geq 1
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
:

\begin{split}[\Psi]_{j+\alpha}&\lesssim[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{1-\frac{(j+\alpha)-(1+\alpha)}{(r+\alpha)-(1+\alpha)}}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{(j+\alpha)-(1+\alpha)}{(r+\alpha)-(1+\alpha)}}=[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{1-\frac{j-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{j-1}{r-1}},\\
[f]_{l+\alpha}&\lesssim[f]_{1+\alpha}^{1-\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[f]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\\
\end{split}

and

[(\mathrm{D}^{l}f)\circ\Psi]_{\alpha}\leq[f]_{l+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}

with this at hand, the product estimates in (
B.1
) and (
B.4
), we can bound:

\begin{split}[f\circ\Psi]_{r+\alpha}&\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma}\left[[f]_{l}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{\sigma_{1}}\dots[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\sigma_{r}}+[(\mathrm{D}^{l}f)\circ\Psi]_{\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\dots[\Psi]_{r}^{\sigma_{r}}\right]\\
&\leq\sum_{l=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma}\left[[f]_{l}\prod_{j}[\Psi]_{j+\alpha}^{\sigma_{j}}+[f]_{l+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}\prod_{j}[\Psi]_{j}^{\sigma_{j}}\right]\\
&\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma}\left[[f]_{1}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\prod_{j}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{\sigma_{j}\frac{r-j}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\sigma_{j}\frac{j-1}{r-1}}+[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}[f]_{1+\alpha}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\prod_{j}[\Psi]_{1}^{\sigma_{j}\frac{r-j}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r}^{\sigma_{j}\frac{j-1}{r-1}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\sum_{\sigma}\left[[f]_{1}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\frac{r-j}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\frac{j-1}{r-1}}+[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}[f]_{1+\alpha}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{1}^{\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\frac{r-j}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r}^{\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\frac{j-1}{r-1}}\right]\\
&\lesssim\sum_{l=1}^{r}\left[[f]_{1}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}+[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}[f]_{1+\alpha}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}[f]_{r+\alpha}^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{1}^{r\frac{l-1}{r-1}}[\Psi]_{r}^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\left[\left([f]_{r}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r}\right)^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\left([f]_{1}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}\right)^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}+[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}\left([f]_{r+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{r}\right)^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\left([f]_{1+\alpha}[\Psi]_{r}\right)^{\frac{r-l}{r-1}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{l=1}^{r}\left[\left([f]_{r}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r}\right)^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\left([f]_{1}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}\right)^{1-\frac{l-1}{r-1}}+[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}\left([f]_{r+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{r}\right)^{\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\left([f]_{1+\alpha}[\Psi]_{r}\right)^{1-\frac{l-1}{r-1}}\right]\\
&\leq\sum_{l=1}^{r}\left(\frac{l-1}{r-1}\right)[f]_{r}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r}+\left(1-\frac{l-1}{r-1}\right)[f]_{1}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}\\
&+\sum_{l=1}^{r}\left(\frac{l-1}{r-1}\right)[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}[f]_{r+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{r}+\left(1-\frac{l-1}{r-1}\right)[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}[f]_{1+\alpha}[\Psi]_{r}\\
&=\frac{r}{2}\left[[f]_{1+\alpha}[\Psi]_{r}[\Psi]_{1}^{\alpha}+[f]_{r+\alpha}[\Psi]_{1}^{r+\alpha}+[f]_{1}[\Psi]_{r+\alpha}+[f]_{r}[\Psi]_{1+\alpha}^{r}\right]\\
\end{split}

where we used the weighted AMGM inequality.
∎

Transport equation and Lagrangian Flow.
Fix
t_{0},\tau\in\mathbb{R}
with
\tau>0
, let
g:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth time dependent function and
u:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth time dependent vector field. We consider the forced transport equation on
\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(t_{0}-\tau,\ t_{0}+\tau)
for a function
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(t_{0}-\tau,\ t_{0}+\tau)\to\mathbb{R}
, initial data
f_{0}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
and forcing
g:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(t_{0}-\tau,\ t_{0}+\tau)\to\mathbb{R}
, namely

(B.5)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+u\cdot\nabla f=g,\\
f|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{0}\end{cases}

and the equation for the Lagrangian flow
X=X_{t}(x):\mathbb{T}^{3}\times(t_{0}-\tau,\ t_{0}+\tau)\to\mathbb{T}^{3}
of
u
, that is:

(B.6)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}X_{t}(x)=u(X_{t}(x),t),\\
X|_{t=t_{0}}(x)=x.\end{cases}

The following result can be found in
[
7
]
.

Proposition B.4

(Transport Estimate)
.

Assume
\tau||u||_{1}\leq 1
. Given
u,g
, any solution
f
of (
B.5
) satisfies:

||f(\cdot,t)||_{\alpha}\leq 2\left[||f_{0}||_{\alpha}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||(g(\cdot,s)||_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}s\right],

for
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Moreover, for any
r\geq 1
integer

||f(\cdot,t)||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim||f_{0}||_{r+\alpha}+|t-t_{0}|[u]_{r}[f_{0}]_{1}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}[(g(\cdot,s)]_{r+\alpha}+|t-s|[u]_{r+\alpha}[g(\cdot,s)]_{1}\mathrm{d}s,

where the implicit constant depends on
r
and
\alpha
. Consequently, the inverse flow
\Phi
of
u
at the identity at time
t_{0}
satisfies:

\begin{split}||\mathrm{D}\Phi-\mathrm{Id}||_{\alpha}&\lesssim|t-t_{0}|[u]_{1+\alpha},\\
[\Phi(t)]_{r+\alpha}&\lesssim|t-t_{0}|[u]_{r+\alpha}\ \text{ for }\ r\geq 2.\end{split}

Similarly, we have the following classical result.

Proposition B.5

(Lagrangian Flow Estimates)
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
r\geq 0
integer. Assume
\tau||u||_{1}\leq 1
, then any solution
X
to (
B.6
) satisfies:

\begin{split}||\mathrm{D}X-\mathrm{Id}||_{\alpha}&\lesssim|t-t_{0}|[u]_{1+\alpha},\\
[X(t)]_{r+\alpha}&\lesssim|t-t_{0}|[u]_{r+\alpha}\ \text{ for }\ r\geq 2\ \text{integer},\end{split}

where the implicit constant depends on
r
and
\alpha
.

For a proof, see
[
4
, Chapter 3]
. The higher-order derivative estimates follow as in the proof of Lemma
2.39
.

Mollification Estimates.
Let
v,\ w,\ f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be time-dependent vector fields with
v,\ w,\ f,\ g\in C^{N}(\mathbb{T}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, define a transport operator
A
by

A=\partial_{t}+w\cdot\nabla

and assume in addition that
Av,\ Af,\ Ag\in C^{N-1}(\mathbb{T}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Let
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
be a convolution kernel with vanishing moments up to order
\bar{m}_{0}\geq 0
, namely, we require:

(B.7)

\begin{cases}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}y^{\sigma}\rho(y)\ \mathrm{d}y=0\ \text{ for any multi-index }\ \sigma\ \text{ with }1\leq|\sigma|\leq\bar{m}_{0}\ &\text{ if }\ \bar{m}_{0}\geq 1,\\
\text{ no condition }\ &\text{ if }\ \bar{m}_{0}=0.\end{cases}

For any given
\ell>0
we rescale the kernel as

\rho_{\ell}(y)=1/\ell^{3}\rho(y/\ell)

and define the mollification
(f)_{\ell}=(f*\rho_{\ell})
of
f
at scale
\ell
to be

(B.8)

(f)_{\ell}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{per}(y)\rho_{\ell}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y

where
f_{per}
denotes the periodic extension to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
f
.

The following is a collection of classical results; see, for example, the deep smoothing operators in
[
26
, Section 2.3.4]
and the Constantin, Weinan, Titi (CET) commutator estimates
[
11
]
, where we adopt the formulation from
[
24
]
coming from
[
33
]
. Note, however, the bounds related to the transport operator
A
.

Proposition B.6

(Deep Mollification)
.

Let
0\leq m_{0}\leq\bar{m}_{0}
and
r,r^{\prime}\geq 0
integers. We have:

(B.9a)

\displaystyle||(f)_{\ell}||_{r+r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell^{r^{\prime}}||f||_{r}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r\leq N,

(B.9b)

\displaystyle||f-(f)_{\ell}||_{r}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r+m_{0}+1\leq N

and the following first- and second-order commutator estimates with transport operators:

(B.10)

\begin{split}||[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
\end{split}

for
0\leq r+m_{0}+1\leq N
and

(B.11)

\begin{split}||[A,[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]]f||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||Av||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||Av||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+2})\end{split}

for
0\leq r+m_{0}+2\leq N
.

Moreover, the following bound on the CET commutator holds:

(B.12)

||(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}-(fg)_{\ell}||_{r}\lesssim\ell^{2+r^{\prime}-r}(||f||_{r^{\prime}+1}||g||_{1}+||f||_{1}||g||_{r^{\prime}+1})\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq r^{\prime}+1\leq N

and assuming in addition
N\geq 2
and
w\in C^{N^{\prime}}
for
N^{\prime}\geq N
, we have the following associated transport estimate:

(B.13)

\begin{split}&||A[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]||_{r+r^{\prime}}\\
&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{m_{0}+2}||g||_{1}+||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{1}||g||_{m_{0}+2})\end{split}

for
0\leq r+r^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}
and
0\leq r+m_{0}+1\leq N
and
m_{0}+2\leq N
.

The implicit constants depend on the specific choice of kernel
\rho
and
r,\ r^{\prime},\ m_{0}
.

Remark 30
.

Note that requiring no moments on the kernel that is
\bar{m}_{0}=m_{0}=0
, one in particular recovers the classical estimates.

Proof of Proposition
B.6
.

Abusing notation, we do not differentiate functions from their periodic extensions. The first two estimates (
B.9a
) (
B.9b
) are standard; see
[
9
, Lemma 7.2]
for a proof. The third estimate is also well known, see
[
24
, Proposition A.3]
.

For any
v:\mathbb{T}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we write:

\delta_{y}v(x)=v(x-y)-v(x).

First-order commutator.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
f
is a scalar function. This reads:

(B.14)

\begin{split}\left([v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]\right)f(x)&=(v\cdot\nabla f_{\ell})(x)-(v\cdot\nabla f)_{\ell}(x)\\
&=v(x)\cdot\nabla\int f(y)\rho_{\ell}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y-\int(v\cdot\nabla f)(y)\rho_{\ell}(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=-v(x)\cdot\nabla\int f(x-y)\rho_{\ell}(y)\mathrm{d}y+\int(v\cdot\nabla f)(x-y)\rho_{\ell}(y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(v(x-y)-v(x))\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\end{split}

We will not provide estimates for this at this point, as the second-order commutator will contain a term of similar structure; we refer to
T_{0},\ T_{1}
below, see (
B.16
).

Second-order commutator.
Using the expression in (
B.14
) we compute:

\begin{split}(A[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f)(x)&=\partial_{t}\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\delta_{y}v(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y+w(x)\cdot\nabla\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\delta_{y}v(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\partial_{t}\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y+\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla\partial_{t}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&+\int\rho_{\ell}(y)w^{k}(x)(\partial_{k}\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y+\int\rho_{\ell}(y)w^{k}(x)(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}\partial_{k}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\underbrace{\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}Av)(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y}_{([Av\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f)(x)}+\underbrace{\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla(Af)(x-y)\mathrm{d}y}_{([v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]Af)(x)}\\
&-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla w(x-y)+(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla v(x-y)]\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\mathrm{D}^{2}f(x-y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x),(\delta_{y}w)(x)]\mathrm{d}y\\
\end{split}

where, we used the following two calculations:

\begin{split}&\int\rho_{\ell}(y)w^{k}(x)(\partial_{k}\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\left[w^{k}(x-y)\partial_{k}v^{i}(x-y)-w^{k}(x)\partial_{k}v^{i}(x)-(\delta_{y}w)^{k}(x)\partial_{k}v^{i}(x-y)\right]\partial_{i}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\left[(\delta_{y}(w\cdot\nabla v))^{i}(x)-(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla v^{i}(x-y)\right]\partial_{i}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\left[\delta_{y}(w\cdot\nabla v)(x)-(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla v(x-y)\right]\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y,\\
\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&\int\rho_{\ell}(y)w^{k}(x)(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}\partial_{k}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[w^{k}(x-y)(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)-(\delta_{y}w)^{k}(x)(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)]\partial_{i}\partial_{k}f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}(w^{k}(x-y)\partial_{k}f(x-y))-(\delta_{y}v)^{i}(x)\partial_{i}w^{k}(x-y)\partial_{k}f(x-y)]\mathrm{d}y\\
&-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\mathrm{D}^{2}f(x-y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x),(\delta_{y}w)(x)]\mathrm{d}y\\
&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla((w\cdot\nabla f)(x-y))\mathrm{d}y-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla w(x-y)]\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y\\
&-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\mathrm{D}^{2}f(x-y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x),(\delta_{y}w)(x)]\mathrm{d}y.\\
\end{split}

We deduce that:

\begin{split}([A,[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]]f)(x)&=(A[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f)(x)-([v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]Af)(x)\\
&=\underbrace{([Av\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f)(x)}_{T_{0}}\\
&-\underbrace{\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x)\cdot\nabla w(x-y)+(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla v(x-y)]\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\mathrm{d}y}_{T_{1}}\\
&-\underbrace{\int\mathrm{D}^{2}f(x-y)[(\delta_{y}v)(x),(\delta_{y}w)(x)]\mathrm{d}y}_{T_{2}}.\\
\end{split}

Estimates on
T_{1}
.
We expand in a Taylor series up to order
m_{0}
and write:

\begin{split}h_{x}(y)&=[(v(x-y)-v(x))\cdot\nabla w(x-y)+(w(x-y)-w(x))\cdot\nabla v(x-y)]\cdot\nabla f(x-y)\\
&=\sum_{0<|\sigma|\leq m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sigma!}\partial_{\sigma}h_{x}(0)(y)^{\sigma}+r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y)\end{split}

where

r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y)=\sum_{|\sigma|=m_{0}+1}\frac{1}{\sigma!}y^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\sigma}h_{x}(sy)(1-s)^{m_{0}}\mathrm{d}s,

note the missing constant term in the expansion, with the understanding that for
m_{0}=0
the sum is just empty. We can bound:

\begin{split}||\partial_{\sigma}h_{x}(y)||_{C^{r}_{x}}&\leq||h_{x}(y)||_{C^{m_{0}+1+r}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}\\
\end{split}

and from this we deduce:

||r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y)||_{C^{r}_{x}}\lesssim|y|^{m_{0}+1}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}),

given the zero momentum assumption on
\rho
, see (
B.7
), and the missing constant term in the series, we conclude that:

(B.15)

\begin{split}||T_{1}||_{r}&\leq||\underbrace{\int\rho_{\ell}(y)\sum_{0<|\sigma|\leq m_{0}}\partial_{\sigma}h_{x}(0)y^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}y}_{=0}||_{r}+\int|\rho_{\ell}(y)|||r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y)||_{C^{r}_{x}}\mathrm{d}y\\
&\leq(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\int|y|^{m_{0}+1}|\rho_{\ell}(y)|\mathrm{d}y\\
&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
\end{split}

where the implicit constant depends on the specific choice of kernel
\rho,\ r
and
m_{0}
.

Estimates on
T_{0}
.
The same argument we used for
T_{1}
can be used here and we deduce:

(B.16)

||T_{0}||_{r}\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||Av||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||Av||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})

where the implicit constant depends on the specific choice of kernel
\rho,\ r
and
m_{0}
.

Estimates on
T_{2}
.
Here we have
O(|y|^{2})
decay despite the pure
C^{2}
norm of
f
appearing. Proceeding as above, we first write:

\begin{split}h_{x}(y)&=\mathrm{D}^{2}f(x-y)[v(x-y)-v(x),w(x-y)-w(x)]\\
&=\sum_{0<|\sigma|\leq m_{0}}\frac{1}{\sigma!}\partial_{\sigma}h_{x}(0)(y)^{\sigma}+r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y),\end{split}

then given the better decay at
|y|\to 0
deduce:

||r^{m_{0}}_{x}(y)||_{C^{r}_{x}}\lesssim|y|^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+2})

and conclude from (
B.7
) that:

(B.17)

\begin{split}||T_{2}||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+2})\\
\end{split}

where the implicit constant depends on the specific choice of kernel
\rho,\ r
and
m_{0}
.

Conclusion.
Collecting the bounds above we deduce that for any
0\leq m_{0}\leq\bar{m}_{0}
we have:

\begin{split}||[A,[v\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]]f||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||Av||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||Av||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+1}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||v||_{1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{2}+||w||_{1}||v||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+2})\end{split}

for
0\leq r+m_{0}+2\leq N
, where the implicit constant depends on the specific choice of kernel
\rho,\ r
and
m_{0}
.

Transport of the CWT commutator.
We first recall the classical rewriting:

(B.18)

\begin{split}((fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell})(x)=\underbrace{\int\rho_{\ell}(y)(\delta_{y}f)(x)(\delta_{y}g)(x)\mathrm{d}y}_{T_{2}}-\underbrace{[(f)_{\ell}-f)](x)[(g)_{\ell}-g](x)}_{T_{1}},\end{split}

we then have

A[(f)_{\ell}-f]=(Af)_{\ell}-Af+[w\cdot\nabla,*\rho_{\ell}]f

and conclude from (
B.9b
) and (
B.10
) that

\begin{split}||AT_{1}||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1}),\\
\end{split}

we then compute:

\begin{split}AT_{2}&=\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}Af)(x)(\delta_{y}g)(x)+(\delta_{y}f)(x)(\delta_{y}Ag)(x)]\mathrm{d}y\\
&-\int\rho_{\ell}(y)[(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla f(x-y)(\delta_{y}g)(x)+(\delta_{y}f)(x)(\delta_{y}w)(x)\cdot\nabla g(x-y)]\mathrm{d}y,\\
\end{split}

we can now argue as in the proof for the commutator bounds above, to deduce:

(B.19)

\begin{split}||AT_{2}||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1}).\\
\end{split}

and gathering the two bounds, we conclude:

\begin{split}||A[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell})]||_{r}&\leq||AT_{1}||_{r}+||AT_{2}||_{r}\\
&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
\end{split}

for
0\leq r+m_{0}+1\leq\bar{N}
.

For higher derivatives, we can follow
[
33
]
and use the fact that

f(x)*\partial^{\theta}\rho_{\ell}=\begin{cases}f(x)&\ \text{\ if \ }|\theta|=0,\\
0&\ \text{\ else \ }\end{cases}

to write

(B.20)

\begin{split}\partial^{\theta}[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]&=[\delta f\delta g]*\partial^{\theta}\rho_{\ell}-\sum_{\theta^{\prime}\leq\theta}\binom{\theta}{\theta^{\prime}}(\delta f*\partial^{\theta^{\prime}}\rho_{\ell})(\delta g*\partial^{\theta-\theta^{\prime}}\rho_{\ell})\end{split}

where
\theta
is any multi index with
|\theta|=r^{\prime}
. We can now commute

\begin{split}\partial^{\theta}A[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]=A\partial^{\theta}[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]+[\partial^{\theta},w\cdot\nabla][(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]\end{split}

The second term using (
B.12
) can be estimated, for
r^{\prime}\geq 1
, as

\begin{split}||[\partial^{\theta},w\cdot\nabla][(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]||_{r}&\lesssim||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}||_{1}+||w||_{1}||(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}||_{r+r^{\prime}}\\
&\lesssim\ell^{2+m_{0}-r^{\prime}}(||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{2+m_{0}}(||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{m_{0}+2}||g||_{1}+||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{1}||g||_{m_{0}+2}).\end{split}

The estimate on the first term follows, arguing as above, where we use (
B.20
) instead of (
B.18
), we deduce

\begin{split}||A\partial^{\theta}[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]||_{r}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
\end{split}

and since the multi-index was arbitrary, summing the estimates, we conclude:

\begin{split}||A[(fg)_{\ell}-(f)_{\ell}(g)_{\ell}]||_{r+r^{\prime}}&\lesssim\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||Af||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||Af||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||f||_{1}||Ag||_{r+m_{0}+1}+||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||Ag||_{1})\\
&+\ell^{m_{0}+2-r^{\prime}}(||w||_{r+m_{0}+1}||f||_{1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{r+m_{0}+1}||g||_{1}+||w||_{1}||f||_{1}||g||_{r+m_{0}+1})\\
&+\ell^{2+m_{0}}(||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{m_{0}+2}||g||_{1}+||w||_{r+r^{\prime}}||f||_{1}||g||_{m_{0}+2}).\end{split}

for
0\leq r+r^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}
and
0\leq r+m_{0}+1\leq N
and
m_{0}+2\leq N
.
∎

Singular Integral Operators.
Let
K:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
be a kernel which is smooth away from the origin, is homogeneous of degree
-2
and has zero mean on circles centred at the origin. Let
K_{per}
be the periodization of
K
, namely

K_{per}(y)=K(y)+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\setminus{0}}\left[K(y+n)-K(n)\right]

and define an operator
T_{K}
acting on zero-mean periodic functions
f:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
as:

(B.21)

T_{K}f(y)=p.v.\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}K_{per}(x-y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y.

The following result is standard; see, for example,
[
7
]
for a proof.

Proposition B.7

(Properties of Calderon-Zygmúnd operators)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
r\geq 0
an integer. Let
T_{K}
be a periodic Calderon-Zygmúnd operator with kernel
K
as in (
B.21
). This is bounded in the space of functions
f
which are
C^{r+\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
and have zero mean, namely

||T_{K}f||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim||f||_{r+\alpha}

where the implicit constant depends on
r,\ \alpha,\ K
. Moreover, let
b\in C^{r+1+\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
be a vector field, we have:

||[T_{K},b\cdot\nabla]g||_{r+\alpha}\lesssim||b||_{r+1+\alpha}||g||_{\alpha}+||b||_{1+\alpha}||g||_{r+\alpha}

for
g\in C^{r+\alpha}(\mathbb{T}^{3})
, where the implicit constant depends on
r,\ \alpha,\ K
.

Remark 31
.

In this work we will often use that
\nabla\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{div},\ \mathcal{R}\mathrm{div},\ \mathcal{R}\mathrm{curl}
and
\partial_{i}\mathcal{R}
for
i=1,2,3
are all CZ operators, where
\mathcal{R}
is defined in (
B.22
) below.

Convex Integration Toolbox.
In this work, we use the following anti-divergence operator:

(B.22)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{R}v=\frac{1}{4}\left[\mathrm{D}\mathbb{P}u+(\mathrm{D}\mathbb{P}u)^{\top}\right]+\frac{3}{4}\left[\mathrm{D}u+(\mathrm{D}u)^{\top}\right]-\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{div}\ u)\mathrm{Id}\\
\end{split}

where
u
is the unique solution of

\begin{cases}\Delta u=v-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}v,\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}u=0.\end{cases}

This operator maps mean-zero vector fields
v
on the torus to symmetric trace-free 2-tensors
\mathcal{R}v
. This is a standard tool in convex integration, which originally appeared in
[
16
]
. We refer to
[
13
, Lemma 2.2]
for the classical statement describing its properties and estimates; since our constructions are local in space, we will need to adapt the arguments to this setting and do so directly in the proof of Lemma
3.22
, which provides bounds for the oscillation term in the Nash Stage.
